The plan below preserves the proposal presented in chat and includes clarifications from document review. Its commands, components, limits, and implementation stages describe proposed behavior, not installed functionality.

1. Goal and selected approach

Your request, rewritten clearly:

Build a shared system that helps AI agents take software work from an idea through requirements, design, GitHub issues, implementation, testing, review, and delivery. Reuse our existing standards, skills, tools, and documents. Add a reliable autonomous runner for approved work. Keep the system simple, avoid duplicate records, and make failures easy to understand and recover from.

The system should work across participating software projects. Each project supplies its own requirements, checks, environment, and limits.

Decisions already confirmed:

  • GitHub Issues remains the authoritative backlog.
  • The runner opens pull requests; a person decides whether to merge.
  • Windows uses WSL2. Linux is also supported.
  • The first version uses your existing Codex sign-in.
  • Show the actual money cost of a run only when the provider reports it for that run. Otherwise show “Unavailable — not reported.” See usage and cost.
  • Production deployment requires separate authorization.

Key terms

Use these meanings throughout the plan, future design documents, and reports. The component table explains each part of the proposed application.

Runner
The complete Python application we propose to build. It coordinates approved work, starts agents, checks results, and records progress.
Component and code module
A component is a part of the system with a clear responsibility. A code module is a unit of source code, such as a Python file or package. A component may use several modules; it does not automatically need its own application or service.
AI model
The engine that generates responses and proposed actions. In this proposal, OpenAI hosts the model; the local Codex software sends requests to it.
Agent
An AI participant with an assigned responsibility, instructions and permitted tools. It performs one of the teamwork roles. Its model is the engine it uses; its chat/session is its conversation context. “Subagent” describes its parent relationship, not a different role.
Role
The responsibility assigned to an agent. The Lead / Orchestrator owns architecture, assignments, and overall acceptance. An Issue Lead coordinates one complex issue and creates its task implementers. An Implementer changes code. An independent Reviewer examines the change; QA independently checks its behavior against requirements. These are agent responsibilities; the controller is the code that supports their work. See teamwork integration for their boundaries.
Skill
Reusable instructions and supporting resources an agent reads to perform a kind of work, such as preparing a PRD. A skill is not an AI model or a running agent.
SDK and CLI
An SDK is a software library that application code calls; here, the Codex SDK starts and manages agent sessions. A CLI is a command interface used from a terminal; here, users operate the runner through devstandards.
Host, workspace, and container
The host is the designated Linux or WSL2 computer. A workspace is the assigned copy of project files. A container is an isolated environment on that host for running processes with controlled access to files and resources.
Run, attempt, session, and agent turn
A run is one approved batch of issues with shared limits. An attempt is a recorded effort to perform an assignment. A session is the agent's conversation and execution context. An agent turn is one submitted Codex request and its resulting work; it may contain several model requests and tool calls. The limits below count observable agent turns, not hidden model requests. Starting a new attempt or session does not reset the run's limits.
Contract
The agreed inputs, outputs, rules, and failure behavior at an interface. A typed contract also defines the data fields and their types so code can validate exchanged information.
Candidate revision and evidence
The candidate revision is the exact code version being checked, normally identified by a Git commit. Evidence is a recorded result, such as a test outcome or review, tied to that version. CI means automated checks run by the repository's integration service.
Approval and reconciliation
Approval permits specified work within stated limits. Reconciliation means comparing saved records with the current files, processes, and remote results before deciding what to do next, especially after an interruption.

One naming system for roles, software and sessions

Recommendation: use teamwork's existing role names everywhere. Keep the role list and technical naming patterns in the teamwork workflow and identity reference. The runner reads and displays those names; it does not create another role system. This proposal applies that wording to its own descriptions and diagrams.

KindUse these termsMeaning
AI responsibilityLead / Orchestrator; Issue Lead; Issue Implementer; Task Implementer; Correction Implementer; Independent Reviewer; QA / Validation AgentWhat the assigned agent is responsible for. “Lead” and “QA” are short labels for the same roles in diagrams.
Optional AI supportArchitect / Planner; Integrator / Release AgentExisting teamwork roles used only when the work needs them. Planning and integration remain the Lead's responsibility; these are not extra permanent agents.
SoftwareRunner; Controller; Agent runtime; Execution store; GitHub connection; Workspace and checks; CLI and reportsOrdinary software components. The runner is the whole application; the other six are its components. The Controller is not an AI Lead.
Runtime and identityAgent; chat/session; subagent; modelAn agent performs a role. A chat/session holds its conversation. A subagent is an agent created under a parent. A model powers an agent. None of these words creates another job role.

Use Lead / Orchestrator as the main role name. The former phrase “Main AI Lead” refers to that same role. Rename the proposed software component “Codex worker” to Agent runtime; use the assigned role name when describing an agent's work. This changes proposal wording, not existing tool identities or the teamwork skill.

In the normal report, show role · repository#issue · task, for example Task Implementer · billing#123 · import. In expandable technical details, retain the existing canonical name, actual tool name and immutable ID, original creator, current supervisor, and ownership version. For example, billing-gh123-import(IL0-IMP) is the canonical name under Issue Lead 0; it is not another role. Never use a friendly label as a tool address or proof of ownership.

Store that mapping in the existing execution record, not in a second registry. Preserve names and counter history on resume. Apply canonical names to new assignments or controlled transfers; do not pretend existing agents were renamed. Historical labels remain evidence. The runner's prompts, reports and diagrams should use this same vocabulary, with compatibility checks against the referenced skill revision.

What “money cost unavailable” means

This refers to the actual charge for a particular run or issue. It does not mean the work is free. The selected approach uses existing ChatGPT sign-in. OpenAI distinguishes subscription access through ChatGPT from usage billed through an API account. Official authentication and billing guidance.

  • Usage: show elapsed time, completed attempts, and any token or account-usage data the runtime actually provides. Tokens are the small units of text processed by the model. Mark missing measurements as unavailable.
  • Actual charge: show an amount and currency only when a supported provider report attributes that charge to the run or issue. Account-wide credits or remaining allowance must be labeled as account-wide information.
  • Subscription payment: the price of an account plan is not automatically the cost of one issue. Do not divide that payment across issues or apply API prices to subscription usage and present the result as an actual charge.

For example, a report could say “Elapsed time: 12 minutes; reported tokens: 10,000; actual charge: unavailable — not reported.” These are illustrative values, not measurements of your account. The runner still applies its configured time, turn, retry, and observable usage limits. A hard money limit requires suitable billing data and enforcement; without them, the runner must report that limit as unsupported.

Recommended foundation

Add a small Python application to development-standards. Use the official Codex Python SDK for agent sessions and use OpenAI Symphony’s scheduling and recovery design as a reference.

Simpler integration choice: adapt the runner to the existing teamwork skill. Use the SDK to start or resume the designated Lead / Orchestrator session; that Lead uses the established teamwork tools and hierarchy. Keep the runner focused on execution control, records, limits, recovery, and authorized delivery. Use one supported agent runtime and the existing records. Do not add a separate agent framework or change shared teamwork rules merely to fit the runner.

The SDK is the proposed foundation, subject to the teamwork compatibility checks. Its ability to run sessions does not establish correct nested delegation, global limits, or recovery. Prove those capabilities in a small authorized experiment before building the full runner around them.

The SDK already provides thread management, streamed events, usage information, cancellation, and sandbox controls. The current published version checked is openai-codex==0.167.0, which includes a matching Codex runtime. Install it in a separate environment so it does not replace your existing CLI or add dependencies to the commit-checking tools. Official SDK documentation, published package.

The research supports this choice:

Candidate What it provides Decision
Official Codex SDK, version 0.167.0 Supported agent sessions, progress events, cancellation, and authentication Use as the execution foundation.
OpenAI Symphony, inspected revision be10a1b Issue scheduling, isolated workspaces, parallel execution, and recovery patterns Reuse its design. Its reference implementation is still a preview; its inspected GitHub adapter leaves dependencies empty, and blocked-state records do not survive restart.
Ralph Orchestrator, version 2.10.1, revision 64040fd Several agent backends, roles, persistent tasks, and loop controls Useful reference, but adds overlapping workflows and storage. Its inspected Codex default uses --yolo; any adopted runtime settings and isolation would need to match the applicable policy.
Snarktank Ralph, revision 6c53cb0 A small repeated-task loop Too limited for this system: the inspected script supports Amp and Claude and accepts a completion phrase as its stop signal.
Gas Town, revision 466aa41 Extensive multi-agent coordination Adds its own task system and many operational roles, increasing maintenance and overlap with our existing process.

Sources: Symphony implementation, GitHub adapter, Ralph Orchestrator, original Ralph script, Gas Town.

The implementation research record will preserve inspected revisions, dates, licenses, dependencies, limitations, and adoption reasons. Symphony and the Codex SDK use Apache-2.0; the three other projects above use MIT. Preserve applicable notices for any reused material.

This is a proposed system. Source inspection has been completed; the runner has not been implemented or tested.

2. The complete development cycle

Keep the existing development document workflow as the central process guide. A run starts with approved work at its current stage. Reuse the existing requirements, design, issues and plans; do not recreate them on every run.

Before and after a runner execution: the wider product lifecycle

Requirements and needed design lead to GitHub issues and bounded authority. After implementation and human merge, confirm issue and feature acceptance. Feature acceptance feeds release readiness; deployment requires its own authority and checks. Maintenance findings return to requirements or the appropriate existing issue. A small fix can enter at an existing issue.

Read the runner cycle in six views

Proposed behavior, not a tested runtime. These diagrams use the existing teamwork roles and the runner boundaries in this plan. Read Diagram 1 first, then follow the detailed views. On narrow screens, scroll each diagram sideways; its labels keep a readable size.

Step-by-step review1. Full run2. Roles3. Build and review4. Accept and close5. Recover6. Feedback loopsResume promptDocument archive rulesTestable design
Step-by-step design review · revision 15 · 10 November 2026

The latest solo source review traced the complete cycle and both feedback directions. Revision 15 makes current-change inspection and relevant history explicit in the resume prompt, aligns selection and diagrams with closure/finalization rules, and clarifies feedback after premature closure. Checks of these edits are self-review; runtime checks remain NOT RUN.

Earlier independent reviews of revisions 11 and 13 led to corrections in revisions 12 and 14: host Git isolation, test-source drift, follow-up backlinks, prerequisite acceptance, rollback history and premature closure. Those corrections and revision 13's component/prompt simplifications remain. The checkpoint preserves the full findings, revisions and evidence; source review does not establish runtime safety.

StepResult and supporting section
1. Start or resumeThe first message goes to the Lead after machine checks of holds, limits and runtime state. Inspect current changes before any relevant-history lookup; preserve uncertain effects. See resume message.
2. Select an issueCheck scope, authority, capacity and dependencies, including accepted prerequisite results and confirmed closure. A later finding follows issue routing, not automatic selection.
3. Prepare and assignRead current task-specific inputs, preserve true supervision, and use separate Reviewer/QA agents for high-risk work. See execution modes and reading order.
4. Build and combinePrefer vertical outcomes and deep modules where they help clarity and testing. Preserve single writers, actual task ownership and combined checks. See design preferences.
5. Test, review and correctUse the frozen candidate with writable test outputs. Route findings through supervisors; close them with action and independent recheck evidence. See feedback return path.
6. Commit and publishCompare committed content with checked content; reconcile uncertain writes before retry. See publication gate.
7. Review and mergeKeep one active PR per issue. Before accepted closure, a failed integrated result can need another PR for the same issue. Human merge remains required. See delivery attempts.
8. Accept and closeRequire agreed acceptance, manual evidence, resolved blockers and current document consistency; confirm GitHub closure. Early closure remains unresolved work. See closure rule.
9. Archive and releaseArchive eligible work records in place; keep living contracts current. Save evidence and confirm release. A failed archive update remains a pending operation. See archive rules.
10. Handle feedback or a stopSeparate same-issue correction, later work and source changes; preserve IDs, holds and limits. See feedback loops and recovery.

Still required before implementation relies on this design: prove the selected runtime's real hierarchy, supervision, capacity release/resume, isolation and stop/recovery behavior in the authorized compatibility experiment. This review did not start that experiment.

Blue boxes: AI responsibilities. Plain boxes: software actions. Warm boxes: human decisions. Dashed borders: conditions to check. Dashed arrows: a return, wait or alternative path. “QA” means QA / Validation Agent; “Lead” means Lead / Orchestrator.

Stage ownership and handoffs

Stage Responsible role or skill Required result
Understand the request Lead / Orchestrator with the user Clear outcome, exclusions, constraints, and unresolved decisions
Product requirements product-preparation Product PRD that describes users, goals, feature scope, and shared requirements
Feature requirements product-preparation Feature PRD with user stories, stable acceptance IDs, rules, and failure behavior
Technical design Lead / Orchestrator using architecture-planning Needed boundaries, interfaces, decisions, and verification requirements
Backlog preparation prd-to-issues GitHub issues with acceptance criteria, source links, and actual dependencies
Scope approval Authorized human Approved requirements, allowed operations, checks, and execution limits
Detailed issue planning writing-plans, when needed Concrete implementation steps that preserve the issue’s vertical outcome
Implementation Lead / Orchestrator for small direct work; dedicated issue/task owners for delegated work Code, tests, relevant documentation, and a structured work report
Verification and AI review Main-chat verification for small direct work; independent Reviewer and QA for delegated work Applicable evidence tied to the exact candidate, with review and validation outcomes recorded separately
Pull request Controller One active PR for the issue, with evidence and clear remaining limitations
Merge Authorized human Accepted change merged under repository policy
Feature acceptance Lead / Orchestrator and designated human owner Coverage of feature acceptance criteria, including any manual checks
Release and delivery delivery-readiness Readiness evidence; deployment and its checks remain separate authorized actions

The controller follows programmed rules to select eligible work from the Lead / Orchestrator's agreed schedule and manage execution; this is what “deterministic controller” means here. The Lead / Orchestrator owns architecture, interfaces, assignments, and acceptance. The controller cannot use a scheduling decision to change those decisions.

Document ownership and locations

Preserve the existing product repository structure:

docs/
├── README.md                     # Current document entry points
├── product/prd.md
├── architecture/
│   ├── overview.md
│   └── decisions/
├── features/<feature-id>-<name>/
│   ├── prd.md
│   ├── design.md                 # When needed
│   └── plans/
│       └── gh-<issue>-<name>.md  # When needed
├── releases/<version>/
│   ├── readiness.md
│   └── deployment.md
├── operations/
├── user-guides/
└── handoffs/

Create documents only when they have useful content.

Information Authoritative location
Intended behavior and acceptance requirements PRDs
Technical decisions and module contracts Design documents, ADRs, and code/schema definitions
Priority, assignment, work status, and dependencies GitHub
Detailed implementation steps Issue plan, or the issue for straightforward work
Verified results Checks, CI, reviewed changes, and release records
Attempts, interruptions, usage, and recovery Runner’s private execution records

Do not introduce another prd.json, local backlog, story database, or editable copy of GitHub status.

Rules that connect the stages

  • Each implementation issue normally delivers one independently verifiable outcome through the necessary layers.
  • Technical prerequisites, migrations, and infrastructure work remain valid exceptions.
  • Stories stay in feature PRDs. Issues reference their acceptance IDs.
  • Plans are prepared near implementation time and reused while current.
  • A changed requirement returns to the relevant PRD/design stage.
  • A changed implementation requires fresh applicable checks.
  • A merged issue does not automatically mean the feature is accepted or deployed.
  • The feature parent closes only after its required acceptance evidence is complete. Manual acceptance remains pending until its designated owner supplies evidence.

Prefer vertical outcomes and deep modules

These are design preferences, not mandatory shapes. Prefer a vertical slice: one useful, independently testable outcome through the layers it actually needs. Prefer a deep module: a small, clear public interface that hides meaningful rules and complexity. A vertical slice describes delivered work; module depth describes an interface and what it hides. Neither requires a particular folder structure, framework, class size or number of files.

  • In the issue and plan, name the observable result, important failure cases and focused acceptance checks. Build the necessary layers together. Technical prerequisites, migrations and infrastructure work remain valid exceptions with their own acceptance.
  • In the relevant design, keep related rules together behind a useful boundary. Avoid chains of wrappers that only pass data through. Keep small helpers, adapters and separate modules when they make ownership, reuse or testing clearer; a deep module is not a large module with unrelated responsibilities.
  • At important public boundaries, use signature, inputs, outputs, preconditions, postconditions, errors and side effects as a checklist. Reuse types, schemas and code contracts; document only what they do not express. Do not create seven repetitive sections for every method.
  • Test behavior through supported interfaces. Keep core rules easy to test without a database or network where practical; isolate actual I/O boundaries and control time or randomness when needed. Add integration or contract checks for the real boundaries. Avoid adding interfaces or mocks solely to match a pattern.
  • Reviewers judge clarity, cohesive responsibility, observable behavior and meaningful tests. They may recommend a simpler alternative; record the reason in the existing design or issue when it matters. Do not reject useful small modules or justified horizontal work only because of these preferences.

For example, an import outcome may validate a file, save valid records and report rejected rows. Its public import operation can hide parsing rules, duplicate handling and transaction details, with focused tests for those behaviors. A small file-reader adapter can still be useful. Model choice follows task difficulty and approved limits; there is no mandatory strongest-model/lightest-model split.

Document archive and reading rules

Archive in place and read by need. Keep stable paths and IDs, following the shared documentation guidance. “Archived” means historical and excluded from the default context; it does not mean deleted, moved to another folder or forbidden to consult. These are proposed runner rules. This edit does not archive existing repository documents or change the shared policy.

One status and one navigation source

Use the document's existing status header or metadata. For documents managed by this workflow, record its status (Draft, Current or Archived), responsible owner/role and last review date. An archived document also records archive date, reason (completed, superseded or withdrawn), and its replacement or evidence/issue link when applicable. Preserve existing identifiers. “Current” means the version relevant to its purpose; it does not grant approval to execute a proposed design.

Reuse the product's docs/README.md as a short navigation page for current requirements, design, operating guidance and active work-document entry points. It contains links, not copied requirements or GitHub status. The document owns its status; any runner lookup cache is derived and refreshed when that source changes. Keep historical links outside the default reading list. Do not create a second document database or archive copies beside the originals.

What an agent reads

  1. Apply current project instructions and the relevant shared policy/skill sections. Reuse versions already loaded and still current.
  2. Read the selected issue, relevant new comments, its current assignment/checkpoint and the specific current documents named in its TaskPacket. Load the needed sections and revisions, not every PRD, plan or handoff.
  3. Inspect the code, tests, contracts and evidence required for the current step. Use the current navigation page only to resolve a missing source; narrow any search to the affected feature or exact reference.
  4. Read an archived source only for a named need: a referenced dependency contract or acceptance result, regression/history investigation, recovery, audit, or an explicit user request. Record the path and reason in the existing task notes. Historical instructions do not grant new authority or replace current requirements.

A missing status, stale index entry or broken reference requires a focused check of that source and its replacement; it is not a reason to load the whole archive or guess a contract. Block only work that needs unresolved information. The controller supplies a bounded context list with path, revision, status, needed section and purpose. The agent may expand it for a concrete need and record why; the list must never hide a required instruction, dependency or safety check.

When resume or review needs recent history, identify the relevant issue from the current assignment, checkpoint and linked issue/PR. Inspect current files and staged, unstaged and relevant untracked changes first, then focused changes from up to five most recent commits related to that issue or its affected paths. Do not use the latest five repository commits blindly or fill the list with unrelated work. If no issue is identified, state that and use the current task's paths without inventing an issue. History explains changes; current requirements and authority still govern. Follow an older exact reference only when needed, recording why. Use read-only inspection; this lookup does not authorize checkout, reset, replay or remote changes.

When archiving is allowed

DocumentArchive condition
Issue plan or completed handoffThe work is accepted and closure is confirmed, or it is explicitly cancelled/replaced; unresolved work has a current owner and linked record, or an authorized decision removes it from scope. Required receipts and evidence are retained. A paused or blocked issue keeps its active plan.
Product/feature PRD, design or operating guideIts subject is retired or a current replacement covers the still-needed requirements/contracts. Completing an implementation issue alone does not archive these living documents.
Decision, review or release evidenceIt no longer needs routine reading and its historical result remains accessible by exact reference. A still-applicable decision or supported-release procedure remains current. Preserve evidence even if its review/implementation is finished.

Before archiving, move any still-needed guidance into its existing current authority, preserving links and IDs; retain historical evidence without rewriting its past result. Update status and navigation together, check references, and record the transition in normal Git history when committing is authorized. If a cross-system update is interrupted, reconcile it from the existing operation record; report archive maintenance as pending rather than repeating issue closure. Do not delete files, sweep folders or apply age-based removal. Destructive cleanup needs a separate explicit request and retention check.

A mistakenly closed issue may reactivate its same plan after the closure is corrected: record the reason and a new revision, preserving archive history. Work discovered after accepted closure receives a new issue and, when needed, a new plan; it links to the old evidence rather than reopening the old plan for new scope. Required historical evidence may be read without reactivating the whole document.

3. Runner design and operating rules

Shared code and project configuration

Place the reusable runner under automation/ in development-standards.

Expose it through the existing devstandards command. Keep installation optional and separate from ordinary standards validation.

Each participating project receives one small, project-owned configuration file:

.development-standards/automation.json

It defines:

  • GitHub repository and target branch.
  • Designated runner host.
  • Requirement and design locations.
  • Trusted approvers and issue-selection rules.
  • Check commands and required CI checks.
  • Approved model profiles.
  • Workspace and test environments.
  • Allowed operations and resource limits.

Do not store credentials in this file. Load trusted configuration from the approved base revision; an agent’s working branch cannot change its own permissions or weaken required checks.

Main components

Keep host coordination in one Python application with the six responsibilities below. These are code boundaries, not six deployed services. Agents and checks still run in their isolated containers; supported parallel work keeps the same capacity rules.

Components and responsibilities
Component Responsibility
Controller Select work, manage stages, apply limits, and coordinate recovery
Execution store Save attempts, ownership, approvals, evidence, and uncertain operations
GitHub connection Read issues and dependencies; publish branches, PRs, and concise status updates
Agent runtime Run bounded agent assignments through the official SDK
Workspace and checks Prepare isolated source, run checks, freeze candidate revisions, and preserve unfinished work
CLI and reports Start, inspect, pause, stop, resume, and explain outcomes

Controller

Host code that applies the Lead's assignments, approved scope, dependencies, saved progress and remaining limits. It schedules permitted actions, checks and independent review/QA, then records results. Findings go to the responsible Lead for routing to the owner. The Controller cannot independently reassign children, change shared interfaces or declare technical acceptance.

Execution store

A local SQLite database and its access code, in the runner's private state directory outside project source and agent containers. It retains assignments, attempts, approvals, usage, evidence references and uncertain actions. Recovery compares these records with actual files, processes and GitHub results. GitHub remains the authority for backlog status, priority and dependencies.

GitHub connection

Host code that reads issues, dependencies and PRs through GitHub APIs and publishes authorized branches through Git. It performs Controller-approved operations and returns observed results, such as a PR URL, for the execution store. GitHub credentials stay outside agent containers; the Controller decides when an operation is allowed.

Agent runtime

Connects Codex sessions and tools through the proposed Python SDK. It starts or resumes the Lead in its container; the Lead and Issue Leads create their permitted agents through supported tools. Sessions carry role, instructions, actual identity and limits. Implementers edit assigned files; Reviewer/QA receive read-only candidate source and permitted test space. The runtime returns structured results, evidence, supported events and stop/resume outcomes. Real hierarchy, isolation and recovery still need the compatibility experiment.

Workspace and checks

Host code that prepares issue source copies, preserves unfinished changes and freezes an exact candidate after writers stop. Configured checks run in separate test containers; outputs and results are tied to the checked content. This prepares environments and runs commands. Independent review and QA separately assess the result against requirements.

CLI and reports

The existing devstandards command on the designated host sends start, pause, stop and resume requests to the Controller. Reports present saved progress, results, usage, blockers and next actions—for example, which action is waiting for permission. Ordinary code formats these recorded facts; no extra AI model is needed.

Example: one issue moving through the components

This example is an ordinary delegated issue with one Issue Implementer. Small direct tasks and complex issues use the execution modes below.

  1. You start an approved run through the CLI with a named Lead / Orchestrator and explicit execution authority.
  2. The controller reads GitHub eligibility and the Lead's assignment, checks resources and permissions, and records the attempt in the execution store.
  3. Workspace and checks prepares the source copy. The Lead / Orchestrator creates the dedicated implementer through the supported runtime; the controller applies the agreed execution limits. The owner acknowledges its scope before writing.
  4. The agent uses the model and permitted tools to change the code, then returns its result.
  5. The controller schedules the configured checks and independent Reviewer/QA assignments. Findings return through the supervising Lead to the existing owner. Required independent rechecks close the findings.
  6. When the required evidence is complete, the controller uses the GitHub connection to publish the authorized PR. Reports show that it is waiting for human merge.

Define these typed contracts for data passed between components. They are data structures, not extra services or AI roles:

  • TaskPacket: the teamwork assignment contract, Section 7, using shared baseline references. Include run/issue/task IDs, plan and ownership versions, actual owner/supervisor IDs, creator evidence, scope and resource reservations, policy/skill revisions, runtime settings evidence, delegation authority, acceptance, reporting route, and limits. On resume, include the current gate, checkpoint and next action, bounded document context list, pending feedback/effects, reusable evidence, allowed operations, saved holds/deadline, cumulative usage and remaining allowance. These extend the existing packet; they are not another tracker.
  • AgentResult: assignment and ownership version, proposed outcome, exact changed snapshot, evidence references, open findings, blockers, and observed usage. Stale results cannot change current ownership or close findings.
  • VerificationReport: candidate revision, check records, separate review and QA outcomes, producer identities/independence, evidence applicability, and integrated acceptance coverage.
  • OperationReceipt: intended external action, authorized caller, issue and delivery-attempt identifiers, stable request identifier, observed result, and reconciliation status. Retries reuse the same request identifier; a deliberately new delivery attempt gets a new one. Record actual agent creation events and parent links separately from names or logical labels.

Agent reports are inputs to verification. They cannot directly mark work complete.

How teamwork and the runner work together

Teamwork defines responsibilities and working rules; the runner provides execution support and enforcement. Keep POLICY.md, the teamwork skill and workflow, and the existing development document guide as the authoritative instructions. This section defines their connection to the proposed runner. Do not maintain a second set of teamwork rules in runner prompts or configuration.

A run must identify one real Lead / Orchestrator session. In attended work this may be the current main chat. Unattended work needs an explicitly authorized Lead session with a proven way to receive queued input and resume. An idle desktop chat is not an available supervisor merely because its ID is saved. If supervision is unavailable, preserve ongoing work at a safe checkpoint and block new dispatch or decisions requiring that Lead.

Choose the mode using teamwork's existing sizing rules
Work sizeHow the runner supports it
Small direct taskThe Lead / Orchestrator performs the work and focused self-review. Record this honestly as direct work. Do not create helper agents just to fill roles. Honor any explicit independent-review requirement.
Ordinary delegated issueThe Lead / Orchestrator creates one fresh Issue Implementer for that selected issue. The owner stays with it through tests, documentation, corrections, and integration support. For work that is not high risk, a nonauthor may combine Reviewer and QA duties while recording separate outcomes. High-risk work uses separate independent agents even when one Issue Implementer is sufficient.
Complex or large issueThe Lead / Orchestrator creates a fresh Issue Lead. That Issue Lead must call the real spawning tool from its own session to create at least two substantive Task Implementers. Task Implementers cannot delegate further. Complex or high-risk work requires separate independent Reviewer and QA agents, scheduled in waves when necessary.

Decision and control boundaries

  1. Design and assignments belong to the Leads. Before delegated writes, the Lead / Orchestrator prepares or reuses the issue design, actual interface definitions, and the conflict-prevention plan. Consequential design and Lead-authored interfaces receive independent challenge before dependent implementation. The Issue Lead may refine internal details; shared-interface decisions return to the Lead / Orchestrator.
  2. Creation follows the real hierarchy. The controller may provide the runtime machinery behind an actual Lead's tool call, but cannot manufacture a child relationship by starting unrelated SDK threads and labeling them as children. Record the spawning caller, returned ID, actual parent link where exposed, supervisor, canonical name, ownership version, and persistent Issue Lead counter. If nested creation cannot be proven, complex issue execution is unsupported; the Lead / Orchestrator must not create the children on the Issue Lead's behalf.
  3. Control requests identify their real caller. Bind a request to its registered runtime session and check its role, assignment, ownership version, and allowed operation. An agent-supplied sender name or approval field is not authority. Resolve commands and allowed resources from trusted configuration; do not provide an unrestricted host-command relay.
  4. Each implementation scope has one owner. Keep exclusive file and shared-resource reservations in the existing issue plan, including contracts, generated files, migrations, test databases, ports, and Git operations. Isolated issue clones do not by themselves separate children inside one issue or external test resources. Give the controller one authorized Git integration identity; agents cannot independently change the Git index or commit history. Stop and reconcile overlapping writes before transfer.
  5. Feedback follows the supervisor route. Apply the checkpoint communication protocol: Lead / Orchestrator ↔ Issue Lead ↔ Task Implementer. Ordinary requests wait for safe checkpoints; the runtime must not inject them into active work as new instructions. The controller may capture usage and status directly for enforcement, but technical findings, decisions, and handoffs retain their intended supervisor route and original IDs. Only the defined urgent-stop cases justify interruption.
  6. Review and QA are separate outcomes. Automated check execution supplies evidence; it does not replace independent QA's assessment against requirements. Cover Lead-authored interfaces, child results, the combined issue, and final integration. A finding closes after acknowledgement, owner action or reasoned response, evidence, and the required independent recheck. Reuse still-applicable evidence according to teamwork Section 14.
  7. Settings follow the existing runtime policy. Use the identity and runtime reference. For Codex-led work, each assignment must stay within both model-capability and reasoning-effort ceilings of the main and assigning agents, unless a scoped user exception covers it. Dot-led work leaves those settings to the platform without model identifiers. A project model profile cannot override these rules. Record requested and established effective settings honestly.

One set of records and execution gates

GitHub owns backlog scope, priority, status, and dependencies. The existing issue plan owns steps and the conflict-prevention record. The execution store described above holds metadata, messages, findings, receipts, and evidence references for that same work. Agents receive only their permitted record views or existing per-sender mailbox files, not direct access to the controller database or credentials. Reports and handoffs are views or references to those records. Do not add a second coordination service, local backlog, or independently edited copy of task state.

Use teamwork's execution gates in that store: Pending → Ready → In Progress → In Validation → Completed → Integrated → Accepted. Record review and QA separately under In Validation. Before accepted closure, a fix reopens only affected gates. After accepted closure, later work uses a linked new issue under the feedback rule. Completed means issue-local verification is complete; opening a PR does not imply integration. Integrated records the actual target revision after authorized integration. Accepted needs integrated checks and requirement coverage. Waiting, blocked, cancelled, and superseded records retain their reason, owner, next action, and history. Deployment remains a separate authorized operation.

Record the policy, skill, and plan revisions used for each assignment. Before affected new dispatch, the Lead / Orchestrator reconciles changed instructions with the runner's supported behavior and trusted configuration. A changed Markdown file must not silently change permissions, bypass a gate, or erase previous decisions. Resolve a real policy conflict before the affected action, using the existing restriction-confirmation process.

Capacity without blocked teams

The proposed limit of four counts all allocated agents across the run: the Lead / Orchestrator, Issue Leads, implementers, reviewers, QA, and correction owners. Count an idle or finished agent until the runtime confirms its allocation is released. The controller reserves capacity before dispatch; Issue Leads receive bounded capacity for their own creation calls. Keep ready review/QA and dependency-unblocking work ahead of additional implementation.

For example, one Lead / Orchestrator, one Issue Lead, and two Task Implementers fill four allocations. Review and QA must run in later waves using proven checkpoint, release, and resumption support. Completed owners remain associated with their issue for follow-up; release does not permit reusing them for another issue. If that lifecycle cannot preserve the hierarchy and required checks, report a capacity limitation instead of skipping roles or waiting forever. Two active issues are allowed only when their actual allocations and shared resources fit.

The documented Codex subagent limit applies per session and excludes the primary agent, so that setting alone cannot establish the runner's global limit. The runtime adapter must account for every primary and nested agent and prevent child creation beyond the reserved allowance. Official subagent settings. This is a required integration check, not a verified feature of the proposed runner.

Readiness and approval

An issue is eligible only when:

  1. It belongs to an enrolled repository.
  2. An authorized human has approved its scope.
  3. Acceptance criteria are observable.
  4. Required documents and contracts are available.
  5. Required checks are known.
  6. Dependencies are satisfied.
  7. No unresolved product decision blocks implementation.
  8. The operation is within the approved permissions and remaining limits.
  9. The selected teamwork mode is supported, a real supervising Lead is available, ownership/resources are ready, and the required review/QA capacity and runtime settings are established.

Record approval against the requirement content, applicable design, execution policy, and allowed operations. Ordinary status comments, tracking links, and completion markers do not change that approval.

The approval record must explicitly cover any planned branch creation, agent/session creation, commits, pushes, PR publication, issue closure/reopening when planned, and infrastructure operations. One bounded authorization can cover the required hierarchy and operations for the run; it does not require an approval for every child or step. Reuse existing scoped authorization without asking again. A ready label, model response, or run command cannot grant missing authority.

Creating an allowed implementation plan does not itself require another approval. If planning reveals a material scope or design change, pause that issue for the specific decision.

The runner may display readiness labels, but a label written by an agent is not proof of human approval.

Selection, dependencies, and ownership

  • Read GitHub’s native dependency relationships with pagination. Do not infer all dependencies from prose. GitHub dependency API.
  • Select eligible issues by configured priority, then oldest creation time, then issue number.
  • A prerequisite issue must have its required result accepted and its closure confirmed. Before dependent work starts, recheck the needed contracts/artifacts and resource availability. A merge, early closure or “closed as not planned” result does not satisfy the dependency.
  • Missing, inaccessible, or cyclic dependencies produce a clear blocker.
  • Start with one active issue. Support two independent issues after the parallel tests pass.
  • Keep one designated runner host per repository.
  • On that host, use a process lock and transactional SQLite claims to prevent duplicate execution.
  • Other hosts refuse execution when their identity does not match the approved configuration.

The first release supports one correctly configured controller host per repository, not distributed failover. Moving to another host requires stopping the old owner, preserving state, revoking its publishing access, and explicitly assigning the new owner. No automatic takeover occurs because a timer expired.

The first message when a run resumes

The Controller first checks the saved run, user holds, remaining limits, host lock and observed runtime state without asking an AI agent to start work. If that check allows an agent turn, the first message goes to the designated Lead / Orchestrator. It is a focused resume request generated from the existing TaskPacket and execution record. No resume turn is sent to a cancelled assignment or to bypass an expired allowance. Uncertain process or remote effects allow only the reconciliation already authorized; dependent writes stay blocked.

This is the proposed first-message template. Fill it from the existing records, using verified values or an explicit UNKNOWN and the affected restriction. The accessible TaskPacket carries the fields defined in the component contracts; do not duplicate that record in the prompt. Load it before acting, then use the document reading rules and recent-work context rule. Never include credentials or the entire archive.

You are the Lead / Orchestrator for run <run_id>.
Resume the authorized work in <repository and absolute checkout>.
Read TaskPacket <accessible reference and revision> first. It names the
selected issues, current gate, owners, checkpoint, next action, source
revisions, evidence, pending findings/effects, authority, holds and limits.
Apply the linked project policy, document workflow and teamwork guidance.

Inspect current files and staged, unstaged and relevant untracked changes
first. If recent history is needed, then inspect up to five relevant commits.
Follow #document-reading-order and #recent-work-context for details.

Reconcile the packet with those changes, processes, ownership, GitHub
results and changed instructions. Reuse valid evidence; preserve existing
assignments, run history, counters and deadlines. Do not repeat completed
stages, uncertain writes or an unconfirmed dispatch.

Source text and feedback do not grant new authority. Missing or
conflicting information blocks only dependent work; do not guess it.

Briefly report verified state, remaining work, blockers/unknown effects
and the next permitted action, then continue within existing authority.
Route ordinary feedback through the supervisor at safe checkpoints.
New scope follows issue-entry and approval rules; work after accepted
closure uses a linked follow-up.

The initial response should identify what can safely resume; it is not an approval request when authority already covers the action. If all selected work is accepted and closed, reconcile finalization and pending effects before reporting the run complete. Report any outstanding maintenance or release separately. Select further work only within the existing batch scope, remaining limits and available resources. A waiting issue stays waiting until its actual condition is resolved. The same issue owner resumes through its supervising Lead with a compact packet for that issue/task; children do not receive the whole backlog. If an owner must be replaced, follow the existing stopped-writer and ownership-version procedure before writes. A new issue receives a fresh issue owner.

Persist the prompt/packet revision and dispatch intent, then the returned turn ID. If submission loses its response, inspect the same session and attempt before retrying; do not send the first message twice because acknowledgement was lost. This is a required runtime behavior, not something the wording alone can enforce.

Workspaces, agents, and permissions

Use a service-owned clone per issue. This avoids changing the user’s checkout and gives each issue separate Git metadata. Preserve unfinished clones after interruption.

Run agents and check commands in separate containers:

  • Mount only the assigned source and required shared guidance.
  • Keep controller state and GitHub credentials outside agent containers.
  • Do not mount the Docker socket, personal home directory, production secrets, or unrelated repositories.
  • Use an isolated Codex profile with supported ChatGPT authentication handling.
  • Keep authentication material out of source, logs, reports, and test containers.
  • Establish effective Codex permissions from the applicable user and project policy before execution. This installation's policy defaults agent-created Codex chats to danger-full-access with approval policy never; narrower presets require an explicit scoped user choice. Record and verify the effective settings before starting work.
  • Keep runner-launched agents inside the approved container boundary, including when Codex uses Full Access. Use unprivileged containers with only assigned mounts and approved network access; reviewers receive the candidate source through a read-only mount. The container and credentials separation must be verified before launch. A runtime preset does not grant access to the host, GitHub publishing credentials, or extra operations.
  • Keep the frozen candidate read-only. When tests or builds need writable files, provide a private test copy of that exact candidate or approved scratch/output directories. QA and check commands use those locations, without publishing credentials or access to another issue's resources. Keep source inputs read-only where supported; declare writable generated/output paths separately. Record the input snapshot, commands and generated-input preparation, and compare relevant source inputs with the candidate before and after each check in a writable copy. A check that rewrites source cannot supply passing evidence for the unchanged candidate: return the source change to its owner, freeze a new candidate and repeat affected checks. Expected generated outputs may differ only under the recorded build procedure and output scope; they cannot conceal a source correction.
  • Make controller-owned Git metadata unavailable for agent writes, while providing the read access needed by approved tools. Verify the actual mount and file permissions; an ownership note alone cannot enforce this boundary.
  • Agents cannot increase their own permissions. Follow the permission-denial process when an action needs access that is not currently available.
  • Limit network access to approved needs.
  • Terminate the whole owned process/container group when stopping work.

When an action is denied permission

  1. Explain the denial. Record the attempted action, affected resource, reason, and the tool or policy that blocked it. Preserve completed work and pause the affected action. Keep credentials out of the record.
  2. Check supported tools and existing approval. Look for an available Codex or platform tool that can perform the same authorized action or request permission through its normal approval flow. Use it only within the user's existing scope and platform rules. Another route must not bypass the denial.
  3. Request the specific permission when needed. Explain what needs access, why, and the smallest necessary change. Use a supported tool's approval prompt when available; otherwise ask the user in the active conversation. Reuse valid existing approval instead of asking again for the same action.
  4. Handle the waiting state. If permission is still required, save a pending decision and wait only on that action. Release idle agent resources and continue independent authorized work. In an unattended run, report the pending request for the user's next review; silence is not approval.
  5. Resume after access is confirmed. Record the user's scoped decision, check that the required access is actually available, and continue from the saved state within existing limits. If the user declines, leave that action blocked and report the remaining work. If the platform or organization forbids the action, explain the limit and the supported administrator route, if one exists; user consent alone cannot remove that restriction.

For example, if an authorized PR cannot be created because the current tool lacks repository access, check whether another approved GitHub tool has the required access. If none does, request the specific repository permission. Do not broaden runtime permissions, container mounts, or network access automatically in response to a denial.

Only trusted, enrolled repositories are supported initially. Do not execute arbitrary public fork submissions.

Use only execution modes that pass the runtime compatibility checks. SDK-managed sessions and native subagents are not interchangeable evidence of hierarchy. Verify actual caller/parent relationships, delivery, permissions, usage, cancellation, and capacity for the chosen mechanism. Unsupported nested delegation blocks complex issues while supported independent work may continue.

Verification, PRs, and completion

Small direct tasks follow teamwork's main-chat verification and honest self-review path. For every delegated candidate:

  1. Stop writers and capture the candidate revision.
  2. Trace the affected behavior.
  3. Run the applicable configured checks.
  4. Obtain independent review and QA outcomes from the required nonauthor agents.
  5. Route findings through the supervising Lead, resolve them within the remaining limits, and obtain the required independent closure evidence.
  6. Confirm required evidence still matches the candidate.
  7. Publish through the controller.

Apply checks according to the project and change: formatting, types, unit tests, integration tests, contract tests, security checks, and acceptance checks where applicable. A missing check is unverified, not passed.

Publish the exact content that was checked. Capture all required source inputs, including relevant uncommitted or untracked files, in the candidate's content identity. The Controller creates the commit and compares its contents with that candidate before pushing. If an isolated project hook or another permitted operation changes relevant content, return through the affected checks and independent review/QA; a new commit ID alone does not invalidate unchanged content evidence. Record the published commit, PR head and applicable target revision. Unexpected branch changes require reconciliation before another write.

Controller-owned Git operations on the host must not execute project or candidate code. Use trusted Git configuration and disable repository hooks and external executable filters for those operations; do not load agent-controlled Git configuration. Run any required project hooks, formatting or generated-input preparation in the isolated check environment, without controller credentials or state, and retain their outcomes as required evidence. Disabling host hooks does not waive repository checks. If that preparation changes source or required publishable content, return it to the owner and capture a new candidate before affected review/QA and publication. Verify this boundary as part of TW-07; a post-commit content comparison cannot contain code already executed on the host.

Keep at most one active PR per issue. A delivery attempt is one candidate-to-PR cycle recorded under that same issue, not a new backlog item. Reuse its branch and PR for routine corrections. After a PR has merged but before accepted issue closure, an acceptance failure may need a new linked PR under a new delivery attempt, with the same issue ownership and cumulative limits. After accepted closure, later work belongs to a linked new issue. Follow repository policy for branch reuse; create another branch only when existing authority covers it. A PR closed without merge still needs the recorded human decision before any replacement.

  • Add correction commits normally.
  • Never force-push automatically.
  • Recheck after corrections or relevant target-branch changes.
  • If synchronization needs a merge commit, follow repository policy.
  • If policy prevents a safe automatic update, record the exact blocker for a maintainer.
  • Unexpected human commits require reconciliation before further writes.
  • A PR closed without merge pauses the issue; the runner does not silently reopen or replace it.

After a human merge, read back the merged revision and issue state. Confirm integrated acceptance, then follow the issue-closing rule: perform an authorized close if needed, reconcile the result, and re-evaluate dependent work. GitHub closure alone is not acceptance.

Checkpoint and release resources during human review waits using the runtime's supported lifecycle. Count allocations until release is confirmed; preserve the owner and resumption record. Other independent issues may use the capacity actually released.

Finish an issue and release its resources

After accepted closure is confirmed, save the final evidence, GitHub result and linked follow-up information. Apply the document archive transition to eligible issue plans/handoffs, keeping current product contracts and needed evidence available. If that maintenance is denied or fails, retain a pending operation with an owner and recovery action; do not claim it succeeded or repeat the issue close. A maintenance retry must recheck current documents and acquire their write reservations.

Confirm owned processes have stopped before releasing the issue's execution claim and resource reservations. Confirm supported agent-slot release before reusing that capacity; keep unconfirmed allocations counted. Block work that needs an unreleased resource, while independent authorized work can use confirmed-free resources within the run's limits. Finalization remains pending until its required maintenance and releases are confirmed. Keep identity and recovery history; never reuse the implementer for another issue. Preserve workspaces and evidence until an authorized retention or cleanup rule permits removal.

At run end, checkpoint unfinished assignments and reconcile owned processes before releasing their write reservations. If a stop or release cannot be confirmed, report it as unknown and keep overlapping work blocked. The final report separates accepted/closed issues, waiting or blocked work, cancelled assignments, and unresolved effects. Ending a run does not erase unfinished work or prove every process stopped.

Recovery and limits

Use SQLite for execution records, outside project source. Store source fingerprints, attempts, ownership versions, actual creator/supervisor/thread/process identifiers, Issue Lead counter reservations, queued messages and finding dispositions, usage, checks, and external-operation receipts. Preserve one teamwork run across recovery.

Before an external write, save its intent. Afterward, save the observed result. Tie publishing receipts to the issue, delivery attempt and action, so a retry finds the same PR while an authorized correction before accepted closure can create a new delivery attempt. Later follow-up work uses its own issue and receipts. If a response is lost, inspect the existing branch, PR, issue state or status record before retrying. Do not assume exactly-once delivery from a network request.

Condition Required behavior
Agent or controller crash Preserve files and assignments; reconcile processes, Git, GitHub, creator/supervisor records, and pending effects. Confirm old writes stopped before transferring scope. Late results from an old ownership version cannot restart work.
Test or review failure Return routine findings to the existing owner through its supervisor. When teamwork's persistent-defect trigger applies, the correct supervising Lead creates a fresh Correction Implementer after a safe transfer of only that correction scope. Preserve all counters and require independent recheck.
Network failure or rate limit Wait with bounded backoff; respect provider reset information
Unknown push or PR outcome Read back the remote result before retrying
Changed requirements Pause affected work and request the specific scope decision
Permission denial Follow the permission-denial process: explain the denial, check supported tools and existing approval, request missing permission, and pause only the affected action until resolved.
Budget exhausted Save progress and stop new agent work
Corrupt execution state Stop dispatch; restore verified state without silently resetting history
Human review pending Checkpoint owners, confirm supported resource release, and report the waiting state and resumption route.
Supervising Lead unavailable Inspect the supported runtime state, checkpoint affected work, and restore or replace that Lead through the recorded ownership procedure. Task Implementers do not bypass the missing supervisor.
Chat continuation becomes due Apply the current policy's rollover rule and open-codex-chat continuation contract. Save the full handoff and confirm creation/dispatch before transferring ownership. Preserve unfinished work, counter/history, budgets, and valid evidence. If the required route is unavailable, checkpoint and report the affected continuation as blocked.

Proposed initial defaults:

Limit Default
Active issues 1
Maximum active issues when their actual capacity and resources fit 2
Total allocated agents, including Lead / Orchestrator, Issue Leads, children, Reviewer, and QA 4
Issues selected per run 10
Run wall-clock limit 4 hours
Active execution time per issue 45 minutes
Agent silence threshold for inspection, not automatic replacement 10 minutes
Agent turns per issue, across roles 8
Agent turns per run, including coordination and support roles 30
Correction rounds 2
Temporary-error retries 3
Consecutive attempts without measurable progress 2

Token thresholds must also be configured and checked across all roles. Report any overshoot caused by work already in progress; do not describe observed-token thresholds as exact billing caps.

These numerical defaults are provisional until the compatibility experiment measures real work. Admit an assignment only when the required hierarchy, correction path, and independent checks fit the remaining limits. If they do not fit, checkpoint and request the needed scoped limit change; do not reduce mandatory roles. At a persistent-defect trigger, use a fresh correction owner only when budget and capacity remain. Otherwise record that escalation as pending. Counters include all descendants and must not be reset by replacement or continuation.

A silence threshold starts inspection; it does not prove an agent or subprocess stopped. Likewise, an interrupted model turn does not prove its commands stopped. Confirm the affected process/container state before releasing write ownership. Apply the policy's compaction and continuation rules using supported events; do not infer advance visibility or ignore required handoffs to keep a run moving.

Human waiting does not count toward the issue’s active execution time. Retry and usage totals survive restarts, new agent sessions, and resumed runs. Exhausted allowances require an explicit increase.

The four-hour run wall-clock limit means one saved deadline from the run's first authorized start, including human waiting, pauses and downtime. The separate per-issue active-time limit excludes human waiting. A timer or resume cannot restart either counter. After the run deadline, preserve work and perform permitted read-only reconciliation/reporting; new agent work and delivery writes need a scoped allowance increase. This default may need adjustment before a pilot that includes a long human PR wait.

Waiting, pausing, stopping and cancellation

Apply a hold to its stated scope. A run-wide hold blocks all dispatch; an issue-only hold allows independent authorized work outside that issue. Record the scope so a timer or another agent cannot bypass it.

ConditionResume rule
Waiting or blockedResume only after the recorded condition is resolved and scope, authority, ownership and remaining limits are still valid. Continue independent authorized work where possible.
User pause or stopA pause stops new dispatch and checkpoints active work. A stop interrupts owned work and confirms process state. Preserve the user's hold; a timer cannot clear it. Resume needs the user's instruction unless that same hold explicitly included a bounded automatic-resume condition.
Cancelled or superseded assignmentNo automatic resume or retry. Retain its history and unmet requirements. Further work needs an authorized current assignment or explicit reactivation with a new ownership version; queued messages and old outputs cannot revive the cancelled version.

Operator commands

Command Purpose
doctor Check prerequisites, access, trusted policy/configuration, and evidence for supported teamwork modes; identify stale or missing runtime checks without launching work implicitly
init --dry-run Preview project adoption
approve Record bounded execution authority for identified scope
run Start an approved finite batch
status / report Show work, evidence, usage, blockers, and next actions
pause Stop selecting work and checkpoint active assignments
stop Interrupt owned work and confirm processes have stopped
resume Reconcile saved state and continue within existing limits
retry Retry a selected, reconciled failure within approved limits

Start manually during the pilot. Add an optional systemd timer only after recovery tests pass. A timer resumes an approved run; it does not silently renew exhausted budgets.

4. Implementation stages

Start with the current preparation and evidence, implementation contracts and unpublished issue drafts. These extend this plan; they are not a second PRD or backlog. Detailed execution starts with the compatibility experiment.

Each stage delivers a usable, testable outcome. Create detailed issue plans near execution rather than writing separate plans for every future issue now. The runtime experiment is an early decision gate: if the selected SDK/runtime cannot meet the required teamwork mode, revise the architecture or obtain a specific policy decision before dependent implementation. Do not hide the gap behind simulated agent roles or a second orchestration framework.

Stage Deliverable Acceptance
1. Align the process Architecture and rule-to-runtime mapping, source references, independent design challenge before dependent implementation, and only necessary changes to existing guidance One clear source for each rule; explicit Lead/controller boundaries; source review distinguished from runtime evidence
2. Prove runtime compatibility A small authorized experiment using the selected SDK/runtime and a disposable repository; evidence for the compatibility checks below Real creation lineage, checkpoint delivery, global capacity, settings, isolation, and stop/resume behavior are proven for each claimed mode. Unsupported modes remain disabled.
3. Preview ready work Configuration, doctor, adoption preview, scope approval, and read-only issue selection Correctly explains why each issue is ready, blocked, or excluded; makes no execution changes
4. Complete one local issue Verified SDK/runtime integration, Lead and issue ownership, isolated workspace, implementation, checks, and independent review/QA One approved issue produces a verified local result without touching the user’s checkout
5. Recover safely Durable state, cancellation, retry handling, usage limits, and restart reconciliation Interrupted work resumes without duplicate execution or reset limits
6. Deliver a PR Controller-owned commits, push, PR creation, CI checks, and human feedback handling One active PR per issue; retries reuse the same delivery attempt without duplicates. A post-merge correction before accepted closure has a new delivery attempt; later work after accepted closure has a linked new issue.
7. Handle dependencies and parallel work Native dependency reads, real Issue Lead-created children, reserved review/QA capacity, global accounting, and shared-resource ownership Supported independent work runs concurrently; dependency, hierarchy, capacity, and resource restrictions remain enforced
8. Close the full cycle Merge reconciliation, integrated issue acceptance, authorized issue closure, archive transitions, feature acceptance, release-readiness handoff, and linked follow-up routing Merged, accepted, closed and deployed states remain distinct. Feedback reaches its owner and returns with evidence; follow-up issues do not inherit approval automatically; archived material is read only for a named need.
9. Package and adopt Optional installer integration, operator guide, recovery guide, evidence report, and versioned release A second test project adopts the same runner using configuration, without copying its code

Pre-implementation preparation and evidence · 10 November 2026

Paused at the user’s request. Live SDK execution and bounded stop/resume were demonstrated; full runner readiness remains blocked on admission control and pending independent review. Revision 19 records the separately authorized follow-on experiment and shared setup guidance. The isolated runtime can authenticate, run the approved model, perform the tested fixture operations and resume a stopped thread. An agent command also reached a separate offline runtime's thread creation without a registered admission call. That observation blocks a claim that all creation is controlled; it does not prove an authenticated extra model turn or make the entire SDK infeasible. What is established, and what still gates implementation
AreaEvidence and consequence
Existing project integrationbootstrap.py:main owns project commands; global_install.py generates the launcher; standards.py owns commit checks. Add an optional automation command through a lazy import in bootstrap.py. Preserve standard-library-only ordinary commands and their existing unittest checks.
SDK and local runtimeThe host Python is 3.12.3 and the existing CLI remains 0.162.1. The approved private environment now contains openai-codex==0.167.0 and its pinned openai-codex-cli-bin==0.167.0; eight packages were installed from a resolved lock with required hashes. The SDK requires Python ≥3.10. An offline container probe initialized the actual 0.167.0 pair and created an ephemeral session with observed Full Access / never settings. No live model turn or real credential was used. Package metadata, official SDK guide.
Control interfacesThe installed CLI generated its protocol schema without a session or model call. It exposes caller thread/turn/call IDs for dynamic tool requests, interruption requests and token-usage notifications. Official documentation describes dynamic tools as experimental. The paired runtime handshake and settings were checked separately; dynamic creation, feedback, stop/release and usage behavior still need their own evidence. Official app-server guide.
Hierarchy and isolationNative subagents inherit parent permissions; role instructions cannot create a separate read-only sandbox. Native delegation in one shared writable container is unsupported for modes that need these distinct access rules. Prove a supported creation route that preserves the actual creator, enforces global capacity and gives each assignment its allowed filesystem/tools. Controller-backed creation is acceptable only behind the real Lead's authenticated tool call, with observed creation evidence and no alternate unguarded spawn route. Official inheritance rules.
Container fixture preflightTwo non-agent probes passed on local Docker 29.1.3 using an existing Python image: 11 assertions each. Assigned source was writable for the implementer and read-only for the reviewer; Git metadata was readable but unwritable; scratch was writable; host home, synthetic host-private data and Docker socket were unavailable. Root filesystem was read-only, capabilities dropped, network disabled and both containers removed. This does not prove real agent isolation, authentication safety, stop/restart, host Git safety or WSL2 support.
Acceptance of the new contractsRevision 16 received an independent design challenge and correction recheck. The reviewer confirmed the hierarchy/isolation clarification and first-run accounting/stop controls, and withdrew an added credential-secrecy requirement after checking the original scope. This is source review, not runtime validation or approval of an unproven execution mode. Historical reviews remain evidence only for their recorded revisions.

The experiment must settle three linked questions: can the selected runtime enforce real creation lineage and a global allocation limit; can it separate each owner's writes and Reviewer access; and can it use supported, isolated ChatGPT sign-in while keeping authentication material out of source, logs, reports and test containers? Use Codex-managed ChatGPT sign-in and refresh in a dedicated runtime profile; the documented device-code flow avoids container browser-callback difficulties. Do not copy a personal profile or extract tokens. The runtime and its Full Access tools share a trust boundary; secrecy of the runtime's own credentials from those same tools is not an existing requirement or a claimed protection. Stronger separation would be a new design decision. Authentication material still stays outside source, logs, reports and check/test containers. Do not invent a token broker from the experimental external-token API. Missing support disables the affected mode or requires a reviewed design change. No complete runner execution mode is currently accepted; revision-19 component evidence below narrows the remaining gaps.

Offline SDK observation: an actual app-server command/exec request could read a synthetic file in its own runtime profile, while host home/socket were absent and Git metadata writes were denied. This confirms the stated shared trust boundary; it is neither a real credential leak nor a full TW-07 result. The first probe attempt had a test-script response-type error; the corrected probe exited successfully and its container/runtime were removed. All complete TW scenarios remain NOT RUN.
